Proofs of all results

B.1
Proofs of Lemmas
VII.1
,
VII.2
and Theorem
VII.1

See
VII.1

Proof.

The jamming condition
P(XZ|ABC)\neq P(XZ|AC)
is equivalent to the conditional affects relation
B
affects
XZ
given do(
AC
), which implies by Lemma
A.3
and the exogeneity of
A
,
B
and
C
that there exists a directed path from
B
to
X
or from
B
to
Z
i.e., that
B
is either a cause of
X
or of
Z
.
∎

See
VII.2

Proof.

P(XZ|ABC)\neq P(XZ|AC)
is equivalent to
(B\not\upmodels XZ|AC)
, by the d-separation property, we must have
(B\not\perp^{d}XZ|AC)
in any underlying causal structure leading to these correlations. This is equivalent to saying that at least one of
(B\not\perp^{d}X|AC)
and
(B\not\perp^{d}Z|AC)
hold. However
P(X|ABC)=P(X|A)
and
P(Z|ABC)=P(Z|C)
imply that
(B\upmodels X|AC)
and
(B\upmodels Z|AC)
both hold. This means that we must have at least one d-connection relation in the underlying causal structure without the corresponding conditional independence in the observed distribution which implies that the causal model must be fine-tuned.
∎

See
VII.1

Proof.

The most general causal structure over
A
,
B
,
C
,
X
,
Y
,
Z
and
\Lambda
where
A
,
B
,
C
and
\Lambda
are parentless while
X
,
Y
and
Z
are childless is one where
par(X)=par(Z)=par(Y)=\{A,B,C,\Lambda\}
. If
\Lambda
is observed, then all nodes are classical. Notice that any causal structure over these variables with the above mentioned properties is necessarily acyclic. We can therefore apply the causal Markov condition
12
12

12

Note that this is not assuming any more than our Definition
V.1
together with the given conditions of this Theorem. This is because in any causal model where all nodes are observed, the graph is acyclic and the observed distribution satisfies the d-separation property, the Markov condition follows as a consequence
Pearl2009
.
to obtain

P(XYZ\Lambda|ABC)=P(\Lambda)P(X|ABC\Lambda)P(Y|ABC\Lambda)P(Z|ABC\Lambda)

(16)

Now, notice that if we had the affects relation
BC
affects
X
given do
(A,\Lambda)
, which in this case is equivalent to
P(X|ABC\Lambda)\neq P(X|A\Lambda)
, then we would have superluminal signalling in the given space-time confirguration since an agent with access to
B
could signal to an agent with joint access to
X
,
A
and
\Lambda
(that can be accessed outside the future light cone of
B
). Therefore, we must have
P(X|ABC\Lambda)=P(X|A\Lambda)
in order to avoid superluminal signalling in the given space-time embedding. Similarly, we must also have
P(Z|ABC\Lambda Y)=P(Z|C\Lambda)
. Plugging these back in the above equation and marginalising over
\Lambda
and
Y
, we have.

P(XZ|ABC)=\sum_{\Lambda}P(\Lambda)P(X|A\Lambda)P(Z|C\Lambda)=P(XZ|AC).

(17)

This contradicts the fact that we have jamming correlations between
A
,
B
,
C
,
X
,
Y
and
Z
which requires
P(XZ|ABC)\neq P(XZ|AC)
. Therefore if we do indeed have jamming correlations in a Bell scenario taking place in the jamming space-time configuration, then the involved parties can superluminally signal to each other whenever
\Lambda
is observed.
∎

A
and

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S)}\equiv\mathcal{G}
for all subsets
S
of
\{A,B\}
. Then denoting
P_{\mathcal{G}}
as simply
P
for short, the first non-affects relation
B
does not affect
X
given do
(A)
(which when written explicitly is:
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(AB)}}(X|AB)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(A)}}(X|A)
) is equivalent to
P(X|AB)=P(X|A)
which is one of the two conditions in
(NS2)
. Similarly, in any scenario where
A
and

are parentless,
B
does not affect

given do
(A)
is equivalent to the second condition of

, namely
P(Y|AB)=P(Y|B)
.

Using the fact that

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S)}\equiv\mathcal{G}
for all subsets
S
of
\{A,B,C\}
. Then denoting
P_{\mathcal{G}}
as simply
P
for short, the first non-affects relation
C
does not affect
XY
given do
(AB)
is equivalent to
P(XY|ABC)=P(XY|AB)
which is the first condition of
(NS3
′
)
. Similarly, each of the remaining non-affects relations translate to the corresponding condition of

′
)
as long as

B.3
Proofs of Theorems
VIII.3
and
VIII.4

See
VIII.3

Proof.

Given the space-time embedding, compatibility implies that
A
does not affect
Y
given
\mathrm{do}(B)
and
B
does not affect
X
given
\mathrm{do}(A)
. Since
A
and
B
are parentless, these are equivalent to the no-signalling conditions
(NS2)
, cf. Theorem
VIII.1
. Hence condition
1
is necessary. Condition
2
is necessary because there are no variables in the future of either
X
or
Y
in the space-time embedding (nor the joint futures of any variables), so the compatibility conditions precludes affects relations of the form
X
affects
S_{1}
given
\mathrm{do}(S_{2})
and similarly for
Y
.

To prove sufficiency, we will show that in any bipartite Bell scenario satisfying conditions
1
and
2
, only irreducible affects relations of the following form are allowed:

1.

A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
X\in S_{1}
.

2.

B
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
Y\in S_{1}
.

3.

AB
affects
XY
.

All affects relations of the above forms are compatible with the standard space-time embedding of a bipartite Bell scenario, since we have
\mathcal{A}\prec\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{B}\prec\mathcal{Y}
. We proceed to show that these are the only irreducible affects relations possible.

Step 1.
We first note that if
S_{3}
affects
S_{1}
given
\mathrm{do}(S_{2})
is irreducible then
S_{3}
cannot contain
X
. This follows because if
S_{3}
contains
X
, then the condition of being irreducible implies
X
affects
S_{1}
given
\mathrm{do}(S_{2}\cup S_{3}\setminus\{X\})
, in violation of condition
2
. By symmetry it follows that there is also no irreducible affects relation of the form
S_{3}
affects
S_{1}
given
\mathrm{do}(S_{2})
where
S_{3}
contains
Y
. Thus, the only possibilities are where
S_{3}
is
A
,
B
or
AB
.

Step 2.
We start by considering affects relations emanating from
A
which are not of the form of I. i.e.,
A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
X\not\in S_{1}
. Since
S_{1}
must necessarily be non-empty and there are only two other variables
B
and
Y
in the scenario,
S_{1}
must be a non-empty subset of
BY
. Now, we write
S_{2}:=S^{i}S^{o}
where
S^{i}
and
S^{o}
are the sets of all setting and outcome variables contained in
S_{2}
respectively. Then consider two cases depending on whether or not
S^{o}=\emptyset
.

•

Step 2a.
(Ruling out affects relations of the form
A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S^{i}S^{o})
where
X\not\in S_{1}
and
S^{o}=\emptyset
) The only such possibilities are
A
affects
Y
given do
(B)
,
A
affects
Y
,
A
affects
B
and
A
affects
BY
. We first note that since
A
and
B
are parentless, d-separation implies that
P(AB)=P(A)P(B)
.
13
13

13

Here we use
P
to denote the distribution
P_{\mathcal{G}}
associated with any pre-intervention causal structure
\mathcal{G}
allowed by a bipartite Bell scenario.
This rules out
A
affects
B
(Lemma
A.2
).
A
affects
Y
given do
(B)
is already ruled out by applying
(NS2)
(cf. Theorem
VIII.1
). To show
A
does not affect
BY
(which implies
A
does not affect
Y
), we start with
(NS2)
which states that
P(Y|AB)=P(Y|B)
and multiply both sides by
P(AB)
. The left hand side gives
P(Y|AB)P(AB)=P(Y|AB)P(B|A)P(A)=P(YB|A)P(A)
and the right hand side gives
P(Y|B)P(AB)=P(Y|B)P(A)P(B)=P(YB)P(A)
. Putting this together gives
P(YB|A)=P(YB)
(which is equivalent to
A
does not affect
BY
since
A
is parentless). [Summing over
B
establishes that
A
does not affect
Y
.]

•

Step 2b.
(Ruling out affects relations of the form
A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S^{i}S^{o})
where
X\not\in S_{1}
and
S^{o}\neq\emptyset
) Observe that for any

and

associated with such an affects relation, the following non-affects relations between elements of

and

are implied by conditions
1
and
2
in any Bell scenario:
A
does not affect
S_{1}
given do
(S^{i})
(follows from Step 2a),
S^{o}

S_{1}
given do
(S^{i})
and
S^{o}
does not affect
S_{1}
given do
(S^{i}A)
(these two follow from condition
2
). Writing these out using the fact that interventions on exogeneous variables do not change the causal structure, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(S_{1}|AS^{i})&=P(S_{1}|S^{i})\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}S^{i})&=P(S_{1}|S^{i})\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}AS^{i})&=P(S_{1}|AS^{i})\end{split}

(18)

which imply
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|AS^{o}S^{i})=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}S^{i})
, i.e.,
A
does not affect
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
since
S_{2}=S^{o}S^{i}
. Together with the above item, this completes the proof that the only affects relations of the form

are those with

. By symmetry, the only affects relations of the form

given

must have

.

Step 3.
Case III. allows

which can be irreducible. Other possible affects relations emanating from

, such as

given do
(B)
, by the arguments in Step 2a.).

This completes the proof.
∎

See
VIII.4

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we use that
A
,
B
and
C
are parentless by definition, in any tripartite Bell scenario (Definition
VI.1
). This implies by the d-separation property (Definition
A.3
) that
P(ABC)=P(A)P(B)P(C)
. Here, we use
P
to denote the distribution
P_{\mathcal{G}}
associated with any given causal structure
\mathcal{G}
associated with a tripartite Bell scenario, and recall that interventions on parentless nodes do not alter the causal structure or the associated distribution (Definition
V.2
).

Necessity
The necessity of condition
1
(namely, that
(NS3
′
)
holds) for compatibility of the tripartite Bell scenario with a jamming space-time configuration follows from applying the equivalence between
(NS3
′
)
and the non-affects relations of Equation (
13
) in Bell scenario: if any of these affects relations were present in a tri-partite Bell scenario, they would be incompatible with a jamming space-time embedding (this holds irrespective of whether or not
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
is additionally satisfied in the space-time embedding).

Now, we turn our attention to the second condition. Consider the case where we are given that
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
. To show the necessity of condition
2
in this case, we first observe that since
\mathcal{A}\prec X
and
\mathcal{C}\prec\mathcal{Z}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{A})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{C})
which implies that none of the joint futures
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{A})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{C})
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{A})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{C})
can be contained in
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
.
Then it follows that in any space-time embedding satisfying the conditions of Definition
VI.3
along with
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
, there are no observed variables or joint futures of observed variables that are guaranteed to be contained in the future of
\mathcal{X}
, of
\mathcal{Y}
or of
\mathcal{Z}
, which means that in order to avoid superluminal signalling, in such a space-time embedding, we must not have any affects relations originating from the outcome variables as required by condition
2
.

Next, consider the case where
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
in a space-time embedding satisfying the conditions of Definition
VI.3
. First, we note that all affects relations emanating from
Y
which satisfy condition
2
(i.e., where
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
, with
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
) are compatible with the given space-time embedding since we have
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
(which implies
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{S}_{2})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
for any
S_{2}
). Below, we show that compatibility of a Bell scenario with such a space-time embedding rules out all affects relations which are not of the form given in condition
2
.

•

Case N1: Affects relations emanating from
Y
which do not satisfy condition
2
.
These are affects relations of the form
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}
and can be partitioned into the cases where
\{X,Z\}\cap S_{1}=\emptyset
and
\{X,Z\}\cap S_{1}\neq\emptyset
. In the former case, we would necessarily have
S_{1}\subseteq\{A,B,C\}
and these affects relations would never arise in any Bell scenario (where
A
,
B
,
C
are parentless by definition) as they are ruled out by Lemma
A.3
(a consequence of the d-separation property). In the latter case, we can further partition into the sub-cases where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}\cup S_{2}
(Case N1.1) or
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}\cup S_{2}
(Case N1.2).
We first reduce Case N1.1 to Case N1.2 using compatibility and then rule out the affects relations of the latter also using compatibility of a Bell scenario with the given embedding.
The only affects relations of the form of Case N1.1 (recalling that we have also assumed from before, that exactly one of
X
or
Z
is contained in
S_{1}
) are:
Y
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(Z\bar{S}^{i})
,
Y
affects
ZS^{i}
given do
(X\bar{S}^{i})
for
S^{i}
and
\bar{S}^{i}
being some disjoint subsets of
\{A,B,C\}
. We only consider the first affects relations as the second one is then covered by symmetry between
X
and
Z
. Writing out the affects relation, we have

\displaystyle P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(YZ)}}(XS^{i}|YZ\bar{S}^{i})\neq P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Z)}}(XS^{i}|Z\bar{S}^{i})

(19)

We can simplify the expression using constraints from compatibility. First notice that compatibility with the given embedding implies
Z
does not affect
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
(for any
S^{i}
and
\bar{S}^{i}
), since the future of
Z
does not contain the joint future of any set of variables. Using this non-affects relation, the right hand side becomes equal to
P(XS^{i}|\bar{S}^{i})
. Plugging this back in, we have

P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(YZ)}}(XS^{i}|YZ\bar{S}^{i})\neq P(XS^{i}|\bar{S}^{i}).

(20)

This is equivalent to
YZ
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
, which can either be irreducible or reducible. If it is irreducible, we must necessarily have
Z
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(Y\bar{S}^{i})
which is ruled out by compatibility with the given embedding. If it is reducible, it must either be reducible to
Z
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
or to
Y
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
. The former is again ruled out by compatibility and we are left with the latter, which is equivalent to
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(XS^{i}|Y\bar{S}^{i})\neq P(XS^{i}|\bar{S}^{i})
. Notice that
Y
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
are precisely the set of affects relations that comprise Case N1.2. Therefore the rest of the proof now covers both Cases N1.1 and N1.2.
14
14

14

Case N1.2 allows all affects relations
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
S_{1}
contains exactly one of
X
or
Z
and
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. The most general affects relations of this form are
Y
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
and
Y
affects
ZS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
, where
S^{i}
and
\bar{S}^{i}
are arbitrary disjoint subsets of
\{A,B,C\}
. The argument for the two sets of affects relations are symmetric under exchange of
X
and
Z
to we focus on
Y
affects
XS^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
.
We had established that
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(XS^{i}|Y\bar{S}^{i})\neq P(XS^{i}|\bar{S}^{i})
, which is equivalent to

P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|S^{i}Y\bar{S}^{i})P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(S^{i}|Y\bar{S}^{i})\neq P(X|S^{i}\bar{S}^{i})P(S^{i}|\bar{S}^{i}).

(21)

Next we use Lemma
A.3
, which implies that (recall that
S^{i}
and
\bar{S^{i}}
consist of parentless nodes)
Y
does not affect
S^{i}
given do
(\bar{S}^{i})
, which reduces (
21
) to
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|S^{i}Y\bar{S}^{i})\neq P(X|S^{i}\bar{S}^{i})
. Then, denoting
S^{i}\bar{S}^{i}\setminus A:=\tilde{S}^{i}
(and assuming for the moment that
A\in S^{i}\bar{S}^{i}
), we have:
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|Y\tilde{S}^{i}A)\neq P(X|\tilde{S}^{i}A)
. We again invoke compatibility which implies that
\tilde{S}^{i}
does not affect
X
given do
(YA)
(i.e.,
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|Y\tilde{S}^{i}A)=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|YA)
) and
\tilde{S}^{i}
does not affect
X
given do
(A)
(i.e.,
P(X|\tilde{S}^{i}A)=P(X|A)
) for any subset of settings
\tilde{S}^{i}
which does not include
A
. This further simplifies our expression to

P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X|YA)\neq P(X|A).

(22)

However, this is equivalent to
Y
affects
X
given do
(A)
which is ruled out, as it is incompatible with the given space-time embedding. In the last step, we assumed that
A\in S^{i}\bar{S}^{i}
. If
A\not\in S^{i}\bar{S}^{i}
, we would instead obtain
Y
affects
X
in this step, which is also incompatible with the embedding. This covers all relations from
Y
.

Case N2: Affects relations emanating from
X
,
Z
,
XY
,
YZ
,
XZ
and
XYZ
Affects relations from
XYZ
would have to affect an input variable which is not possible due to the parentless nature of these variables in the Bell scenario. Affects relations from
X
,
Z
and
XZ
are ruled out by compatibility of the Bell scenario with the given embedding (there are no subsets whose joint future is contained in that of
X
,

or
XZ
). Affects relations from

(if they exist), must be of the form

affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
and can be either irreducible or reducible. If irreducible, this means that
X
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2}Y)
which we have already ruled out by compatibility. If reducible, it must be reduced to either one of the irreducible relations
X
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
or
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
(where
X\not\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}
). The former, we have already ruled out and in the latter case, and the latter cannot possibly satisfy condition
2
and are therefore also ruled out by earlier arguments.

This completes the proof for the necessary part.

Sufficiency
We start with the case where
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
. To prove sufficiency in this case, we will show that in any tripartite Bell scenario satisfying conditions
1
and
2
of the current theorem, the only irreducible affects relations that are allowed are the following.

•

Case S1:

S1.1

A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
X\in S_{1}

S1.2

C
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
Z\in S_{1}

S1.3

AC
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}

•

Case S2:

S2.1

B

S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
Y\in S_{1}
or
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
or both

S2.2

(S_{2})

\{X,Y\}\subseteq S_{1}
or
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
or both

S2.3

S_{1}

(S_{2})
where
\{Y,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
or
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
or both

Case S3:

ABC
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}

Notice that all affects relations of the above form are compatible with the jamming space-time embedding of a tripartite Bell scenario, since we have
\mathcal{A}\prec\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{B}\prec Y
,
\mathcal{C}\prec\mathcal{Z}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{B})
. As reducible affects relations (even if they exist) do not imply any additional compatibility constraints, any scenario where these are the only irreducible affects relations would be compatible with the given space-time embedding and consequently, not lead to superluminal signalling. Therefore showing that conditions
1
and
2
imply that the affects relations of Cases S1 to S3. are the only allowed irreducible affects relations, would complete this proof for the case where
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
.

Step 1.
Using the same argument as in Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
VIII.3
, we conclude that if
S_{3}
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})

is irreducible, then
S_{3}
cannot contain
X
,

or

(any affects relation of this form where

contains one of these outcome variables would be rendered reducible to an affects relation without these variables in the first argument of the affects relation, due to condition
2
of the theorem). Thus the only possibilities for irreducible affects relations are those where
S_{3}
is a non-empty subset of
\{A,B,C\}
, and there are 7 such possibilities.

Step 2.
The proof for the cases where
S_{3}=A
(Case S1.1) and
S_{3}=C
(Case S1.2) is identical to that of Step 2 in Theorem
VIII.3
. [Cases where

affects

given do
(S_{2})
where

and

only contains inputs are excluded using NS3
′
, and the extension to the case of general

uses Step 2b of the previous proof.]

For the case where

(Case S1.3), we have irreducible affects relations of the form

affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
. By the definition of irreducibility this implies that
A
affects
S_{1}
given do
(CS_{2})
and

affects
S_{1}
given do
(AS_{2})
, which are affects relations covered by Cases S1.1 and S1.2 respectively (which we have already addressed). By the condition of Case S1.1, we must have
X\in S_{1}
and by the condition of Case S1.1, we must have
X\in S_{1}
for such an affects relation to hold. This implies that the only irreducible affects relations emanating from
S_{3}=AC
are those of the form of Case S1.3.
15
15

15

While affects relations such as
AC
affects
X
(with possible conditioning on other interventions) are possible, they would not be irreducible since
C
does not affect
X
(even when conditioning on interventions on other variables).
This covers all of Case S1, which concerns affects relations emanating from sets of variables not containing
B
.

Step 3.
We now consider Cases S2 and S3 which involve the variable
B
, which has a special role in this scenario (due to the possibility of jamming). The proof in this case is also similar to that of Step 2, but we repeat it for completeness. Consider
S_{3}=B
, and consider affects relations emanating from
S_{3}
which are not of the form of S2.1 i.e., where
B
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
with

and
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}
. As in the proof of Theorem
VIII.3
we partition

where

and

consists only of setting and outcome variables respectively, and we treat the two cases

and

separately.

•

Step 3a.
(Ruling out affects relations of the type
B
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S^{i}S^{o})
with
Y\not\in S_{1}
,
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}
and
S^{o}=\emptyset
) The only such possibilities are: affects relations where
S_{1}
only contains settings (these are ruled out using the free choice of settings, and the d-separation property),
B
affects
X
,
B

Z
, and the last two affects relations with the conditioning on do
(A)
, do
(C)
or do

. Consider

(AC)
. Using Theorem

we know that
(NS3
′
)
(condition

BC
does not affect
X
given do
(A)
and

does not affect
XY
given do
(AB)
. The latter immediately implies that

does not affect

given do
(AB)
and together with
P(ABC)=P(A)P(B)P(C)
, we can easily verify that this gives us

does not affect

given do
(A)
, which is equivalent to

in a Bell scenario. Using this in
BC
does not affect
X
given do
(A)
, which is equivalent to

, we have

, which is equivalent to
B
does not affect
X
given do
(AC)
. It is straightforward to verify that this also implies
B
does not affect

given do
(A)
,
B
does not affect

given do
(C)
and
B
does not affect

. By symmetry, we can rule out affects relations

(given do

, do

, do

Step 3b.
(Ruling out affects relations of the type
B
affects

given do
(S^{i}S^{o})
with

,
\{X,Z\}\not\subseteq S_{1}
and

) Observe that for any

and

associated with such an affects relation, the following non-affects relations are implied by conditions

and

in any Bell scenario:

does not affect

given do

that forbids affects relations emanating from sets of outcome variables). Writing down these three non-affects relations explicitly, while using the fact that interventions on any set

(which consists only of settings) does not change the causal structure, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(S_{1}|BS^{i})&=P(S_{1}|S^{i})\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}S^{i})&=P(S_{1}|S^{i})\\
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}AS^{i})&=P(S_{1}|BS^{i})\end{split}

(23)

The above implies that
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}BS^{i})=P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(S^{o})}}(S_{1}|S^{o}S^{i})
which, in any Bell scenario, is equivalent to
B
does not affect
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
since
S_{2}=S^{o}\cup S^{i}
. Together with Step 3a, this completes the proof for Case S2.1.

The proofs for Cases S2.2 and S2.3 follow from Cases S1 and S2.1 and the definition of irreducibility. For Case S2.2, we consider irreducible affects relations of the form

given do
(S_{2})

and

must hold and the conditions of Case S2.2 tell us that either

or

or

for an irreducible affects relation

affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
to hold, which is precisely the condition of Case S2.2. Case S2.3 is similarly covered by using the definition of irreducibility and referring to the previous Cases S1.2 and S2.1.

Therefore, using conditions 1 and 2, we have shown that the only irreducible affects relations are of the form of those listed in Cases S1 to S3. All of these are compatible with any space-time embedding that respects the jamming configuration (Definition
VI.3
) and
\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\not\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})
.

\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{X})\cap\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{Y})

′

. In Step 1, using condition

instead of condition

B.4
Proof of Theorem
VIII.5

See
VIII.5

Proof.

Consider the bipartite Bell scenario. Condition
2
of Theorem
VIII.3
is sufficient for ruling out affects causal loops because it restricts all affects relations to start from either
A
or
B
or
AB
, but these variables are parentless so no affects relation can end on
A
,
B
or
AB
, so there cannot be an affects causal loop. The proof of sufficiency of condition
2
of Theorem
VIII.4
for the tripartite scenario is analogous. For condition
2
of Theorem
VIII.4
, there is are additional possible affects relations of the form
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
. Here,
S_{1}
could contain the setting variables,
A
,
B
or
C
. However, even such affects relations cannot allow us to infer a causal influence ending on
A
,
B
or
C
as these are parentless nodes in a Bell scenario. The fact that no causal influence can end on
A
,
B
or
C
, together with the fact that no affects relations can start from
X
or
Z
means that there cannot be an affects causal loop in any set of affects relations satisfying condition
2
.

To show the sufficiency of condition
2
of Theorem
VIII.4
for ruling out affects loops, notice that
2
also allows affects relations of the form
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
. This allows us to have affects relations from
Y
to
B
, for instance where
B\in S_{1}
, and one might expect that together with
B
affects
Y
(which is also allowed), this might lead to an affects loop. However, while
B
affects
Y
allows us to infer that
B
is a cause of
Y
(Lemma
A.2
), no affects relation emanating from
Y
could allow us to infer that
Y
is a cause of
B
, since
B
is a parentless node and has no causes. The only causal loops (if present) could be between outcome variables, however as there are no affects relations emanating from
X
or
Z
, having the affects relations
Y
affects
S_{1}
given do
(S_{2})
where
\{X,Z\}\subseteq S_{1}
alone are insufficient to infer the existence of causal loops i.e., any causal loops (if they exist) cannot be affects causal loops.

The insufficiency of these conditions for ruling out hidden causal loops is established by Example
B.2
(originally introduced in
VilasiniColbeckPRA
). This example presents a hidden causal loop which satisfies
(NS2)
,
(NS3)
and
(NS3
′
)
. Moreover, as the example only involves two non-trivial outcomes
X
and
Y
without any affects relations emanating from them, it satisfies the second conditions of Theorems
VIII.3
and
VIII.4
(both
2
and
2
in the latter case).
∎

are insufficient for ruling out hidden causal loops in Bell scenarios) (c) Example
B.3
(which shows that
(NS2)
/
(NS3)
are not necessary for ruling out causal loops in Bell scenarios) (d) Example
B.4
(which shows that the second condition of Theorems
VIII.3
and
VIII.4
are not necessary for ruling out causal loops in Bell scenarios) (e) Space-time embedding of the observed variables (circled) in all of these examples. We recall that in causal structures, a dashed causal arrow
N_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}{\mathrel{}\mathrel{\mathop{\dabar@\dabar@}\limits}\mathrel{\mathchar 0\symAMSa 04B}{}}}N_{2}
as well as a solid causal arrow
N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{2}
denote that
N_{1}
is a direct cause of
N_{2}
, but the dashed arrow indicates that
N_{1}
does not affect
N_{2}
while the solid arrow indicates that
N_{1}
affects
N_{2}
in the given causal model.

Pearl2009
;
Forre2017
, this preserves the causal mechanisms of the edges that are not removed and fixes the variable being intervened on to some value. Doing so, we see that
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(X)}}(Y=1|X=1)=1
since we have the
Y=X
causal mechanism here, while
P_{\mathcal{G}}(Y=1)=0
, this means that
X
affects
Y
. Similarly, we
P_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{do}(Y)}}(X=1|Y=2)=1
since we have the
X=2Y
mod
3
causal mechanism here, while
P_{\mathcal{G}}(X=1)=0
, which means that we also have
Y
affects
X
. These affects relations form an affects causal loop as they allow us to infer a causal cycle between the RVs
X
and
Y
. The same example can be extended to establish the insufficiency of
(NS3)
(and hence the weaker condition
(NS3
′
)
) for ruling out affects causal loops in tripartite Bell scenarios.

}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.94 L 0 3.94 L 2.08 5.25 L 6.23 2.62 L 10.38 5.25 L 12.45 3.94 L 12.45 3.94 L 23.07 3.94}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.41183pt}{2.84544pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.2 C 0.14 1.38 1.66 0.14 2.08 0 C 1.66 -0.14 0.14 -1.38 0 -2.2}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}Y
(Figure
9b
). Suppose all nodes correspond to classical and binary variables,
X
and
Y
are observed,
\Lambda
is unobserved and the causal model is such that
\Lambda
is uniformly distributed,
X=Y\oplus\Lambda
,
Y=X\oplus\Lambda
. One can verify that
X
and
Y
are uncorrelated,
X
does not affect
Y
and
Y
does not affect
X
(using the methods explained in the previous example). However we have a causal loop between
X
and
Y
by construction. This is a hidden causal loop and not an affects causal loops because the the observable affects relations of the model are trivially replicated in an acyclic model where
X
and
Y
have no causal relations. Moreover this model trivially satisfies the non-signalling conditions
(NS2)
,
(NS3)
and
(NS3
′
)
when we introduce dummy variables corresponding to the settings (which does not change any of the above properties).

The following counter-examples prove the latter statement of Theorem
VIII.6
.

Example B.3

(
(NS2)
/
(NS3)
are not necessary for ruling out causal loops in Bell scenarios)
.

Consider a causal structure of a Bell scenario where
A\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to4.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

(Figure

). This would violate
(NS2)
,
(NS3)
and
(NS3
′
)
since Alice’s (freely chosen) setting is correlated with Bob’s outcome enabling them to communicate. However, this causal structure is acyclic and therefore has no causal loops (neither affects nor hidden loops).

Example B.4

(Second conditions of Theorems

and

are not necessary for ruling out causal loops in Bell scenarios)
.

(Figure

). This would trivially satisfy

,

and

′
)
as the settings are causally disconnected to all outcomes, and moreover is an acyclic causal structure and hence has no causal loops of any kind. However, we have

which violates conditions 2 of Theorems

and

as well as condition

of Theorem
VIII.4
.

Appendix C
Space-time positions compatible with jamming

In this section we outline which space-time locations are compatible with jamming and how this depends on the number of spatial dimensions. To do so we discuss when the intersection of two lightcones is contained within a third for arbitrary space-time points. This can be applied to jamming with the notation of the rest of the paper by taking the space-time location of
X
to be
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
, that of
B
to be
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
and that of
Z
to be
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})
. We begin with some notation.

C.1
Lightcone notation

Given an event
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
, its associated
lightcone

L({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
is the set of points satisfying
({\bf x}-{\bf x}_{A}).({\bf x}-{\bf x}_{A})-(t-t_{A})^{2}\leq 0
, or
|{\bf x}-{\bf x}_{A}|^{2}\leq(t-t_{A})^{2}
(we take the speed of light to be
1
for convenience). Its
future lightcone
, denoted
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
is the subset of the lightcone with
t>t_{A}
. We will be interested in the intersection of lightcones. Firstly, a small observation: the future lightcone of any point in a lightcone remains in that lightcone.

Lemma C.1
.

Given an event
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
and a second event in its future lightcone
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
, we have
L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
.

Proof.

Since
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
, we have
|{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{A}|^{2}\leq(t_{B}-t_{A})^{2}
. Let
({\bf x},t)\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
. It follows that
|{\bf x}-{\bf x}_{B}|\leq(t-t_{B})^{2}
with
t_{A}<t_{B}<t
. Then, using the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle|{\bf x}-{\bf x}_{A}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|{\bf x}-{\bf x}_{B}|+|{\bf x}_{B}-{\bf x}_{A}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(t-t_{B})+(t_{B}-t_{A})=t-t_{A}\,,

which implies
({\bf x},t)\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
. Since
({\bf x},t)
was an arbitrary point in
L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
it follows that
L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
.
∎

We use the notation
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}:=\{{\bf x}:({\bf x},t)\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\}
, for the set of possible spatial coordinates of points in the lightcone
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
with fixed time coordinate
t
.

C.2
(1+1)
D

In
(1+1)
D the intersection of two future lightcones is itself a lightcone, eminating from a unique point of earliest interception.

Lemma C.2
.

Given two events
(x_{A},t_{A})
and
(x_{C},t_{C})
in
(1+1)
D, there is a unique point
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
of earliest interception of their future lightcones, and
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})=L_{>}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
(x_{C},t_{C})\in L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})
. By Lemma
C.1
,
L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\subseteq L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})
, and hence
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})=L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
, i.e., the statement holds with
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})=(x_{C},t_{C})
. The case
(x_{A},t_{A})\in L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
is analogous.

In the alternative case, without loss of generality, assume
x_{A}<x_{C}
and
t_{A}<t_{C}
. To not be in the first case we require
x_{C}-x_{A}>t_{C}-t_{A}
. The edges of the lightcone
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})
are given by
x=x_{A}\pm(t-t_{A})
, and those of
L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
are given by
x=x_{C}\pm(t-t_{C})
. The only two that meet in the future are
x=x_{A}+t-t_{A}
and
x=x_{C}-t+t_{C}
, which meet at
x^{\prime}=(x_{C}+x_{A}+t_{C}-t_{A})/2
and
t^{\prime}=(x_{C}-x_{A}+t_{C}+t_{A})/2
. Any event with a time coordinate smaller than this cannot be in both lightcones, and since
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
is in both lightcones, by Lemma
C.1
it follows that
L_{>}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\subseteq L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
.

Now consider an arbitrary point
(x,t)
in
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
. Such a point must satisfy
x\leq x_{A}+t-t_{A}
and
x\geq x_{C}-t+t_{C}
. Thus,

\displaystyle x-x^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle x_{A}+t-t_{A}-x^{\prime}=t-(x_{C}-x_{A}+t_{A}+t_{C})/2=t-t^{\prime}

\displaystyle x-x^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle x_{C}-t+t_{C}-x^{\prime}=-(t-(x_{C}-x_{A}+t_{A}+t_{C})/2)=-(t-t^{\prime})\,,

which imply
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq t-t^{\prime}
, i.e.,
(x,t)\in L_{>}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
.

Putting all these together, we have
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})=L_{>}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
, as required.
∎

Next we show that consideration of a single time coordinate is enough to establish whether the intersection of two lightcones is contained in a third.

Lemma C.3
.

Consider three arbitrary events
(x_{A},t_{A})
,
(x_{B},t_{B})
and
(x_{C},t_{C})
.

1.

If for some time
t>t_{B}
we have
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
, then
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\subseteq L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})
.

2.

If for some time
t>t_{B}
we have
L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
, then
L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})\subseteq L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
.

Proof.

Suppose
(x_{A},t_{A})
and
(x_{C},t_{C})
are spacelike separated and, without loss of generality, suppose
x_{A}<x_{C}
and
t_{A}<t_{C}
so that the condition of spacelike separation is
x_{C}-x_{A}>t_{C}-t_{A}
.

The set of points in
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
is given by
x\in[x_{C}-t+t_{C},x_{A}+t-t_{A}]
, and the set of points in
L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
is given by
x\in[x_{B}-t+t_{B},x_{B}+t-t_{B}]
and we assume this set is not empty. For
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
we require
x_{C}-t+t_{C}\leq x_{B}-t+t_{B}
and
x_{B}+t-t_{B}\leq x_{A}+t-t_{A}
. However, if these hold for a particular value of
t
, they hold for all
t
(since
t
cancels from both equations). Likewise for the other direction. This proves the case where
(x_{A},t_{A})
and
(x_{C},t_{C})
are spacelike separated. The case where they are not also follows analogously.
∎

Applying Lemma
C.3
with
t=t^{\prime}
we have the corollary that the intersection of
L_{>}(x_{A},t_{A})
and
L_{>}(x_{C},t_{C})
is contained in
L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})
if and only if
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in L_{>}(x_{B},t_{B})
.

C.3
(d+1)
D for
d\geq 2

With more than one spatial dimension it is no longer the case that the intersection of two lightcones is a lightcone. [For instance, in
(2+1)
D, for a fixed time a lightcone is a circle, but the intersection of two circles is not a circle.] The analogue of Lemma
C.2
hence does not hold in higher dimensions.

Instead of Lemma
C.3
, the following lemma shows that consideration of a single time coordinate is not sufficient to establish that the intersection of two lightcones is contained within a third.

Before this we introduce two pieces of notation:
{\bf 0}_{d}
is shorthand for
d
zeros, and
{\bf x}_{d-n}
is the subvector of
{\bf x}
with the first
n
components removed, e.g., in dimension
d=3
, with
{\bf x}=(x,y,z)
,
{\bf x}_{2}
means
(y,z)
.

Lemma C.4
.

There exist three events
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
,
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
and
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})
, and two times
t
and
t^{\prime}>t
such that
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
but
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t^{\prime}}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t^{\prime}}\nsubseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We can show this with an example: take
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})=((-a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
,
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})=((a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
and
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})=((0,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),a/2)
. The set
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
is then nonempty if and only if
t>a
. For
t>a
, the points in
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
furthest from
{\bf x}={\bf 0}_{d}
are the maxima of
|{\bf x}|^{2}
subject to
|{\bf x-x}_{A}|^{2}\leq t^{2}
and
|{\bf x-x}_{C}|^{2}\leq t^{2}
. The objective function has no global maximum, so the maximum must occur on the boundary. The first boundary,
(x+a)^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}
, meets the second,
(x-a)^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}
, when
x=0
and
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}-a^{2}
. On the first boundary we have
x^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}-2ax-a^{2}
, and
x\geq 0
to also be inside the second region. Thus, the maximum value of
x^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}
is
t^{2}-a^{2}
, which occurs at
x=0
and
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}-a^{2}
.

The points with
x=0
and
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}-a^{2}
are inside the region
L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
if and only if
t^{2}-a^{2}\leq(t-t_{B})^{2}
, which for
t_{B}=a/2
is equivalent to
t\leq 5a/4
. Hence, for
1<t\leq 5a/4
we have
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
. However, if we take
t^{\prime}>5a/4
the points with
x=0
and
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=(t^{\prime})^{2}-a^{2}
are inside
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t^{\prime}}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t^{\prime}}
but outside
L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t^{\prime}}
.
∎

We would like to know when the intersection of two lightcones is contained within a third. This breaks down into two cases treated separately in the following two lemmas (the first of which is most relevant for jamming).

Lemma C.5
.

Let
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})=((-a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
,
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})=((a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
and
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})=((x_{B},y_{B},{\bf 0}_{d-2}),t_{B})
for some
-a\leq x_{B}\leq a
.
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
if and only if
t_{B}\leq-|y_{B}|
.

Note that the form of the chosen points is not losing generality: for any two spacelike separated events we can find a frame where they have coordinates of the form given for
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})
and
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})
. Similarly, because any three points lie on a 2D plane we can also choose
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
to have the form given.

In addition, the condition
t_{B}\leq-|y_{B}|
is equivalent to saying that an event with
t=0
on the line between
{\bf x}_{A}
and
{\bf x}_{B}
lies in the future lightcone of
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
.

Proof.

For
t>a
, the part of the boundary of
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
that is the same as the boundary of
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}
, is given by
x=\sqrt{t^{2}-|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}}-a
, where
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}\leq t^{2}-a^{2}
. [Note that the two lightcones meet when
x=0
and
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}=t^{2}-a^{2}
.]

We want to calculate the maximum distance a point on this boundary could be from
{\bf x}_{B}
. The square of this distance is

\displaystyle(x-x_{B})^{2}+(y-y_{B})^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{2}+|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}+x_{B}^{2}+y_{B}^{2}-2xx_{B}-2yy_{B}

(25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{2}-2ax-a^{2}+x_{B}^{2}+y_{B}^{2}-2xx_{B}-2yy_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{2}+x_{B}^{2}+y_{B}^{2}-2yy_{B}-a^{2}-2(a+x_{B})(\sqrt{t^{2}-|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}}-a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{2}+x_{B}^{2}+y_{B}^{2}-2yy_{B}+a^{2}+2ax_{B}-2(a+x_{B})\sqrt{t^{2}-y^{2}-|{\bf x}_{d-2}|^{2}}\,.

Without loss of generality we can take
x_{B}\leq 0
and
y_{B}\geq 0
. For
-a\leq x_{B}\leq 0
the maximum distance occurs when
y=-\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}}
,
|{\bf x}_{d-2}|^{2}=0
and hence
x=0
. This square distance is

x_{B}^{2}+\left(y_{B}+\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}}\right)^{2}.

(26)

Hence
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}
if and only if
x_{B}^{2}+(y_{B}+\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}})^{2}\leq(t-t_{B})^{2}
, or equivalently,

2tt_{B}+2y_{B}\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}}\leq t_{B}^{2}+a^{2}-x_{B}^{2}-y_{B}^{2}.

(27)

We want to find values of
t_{B}
such that this holds for all
t>a
. For large enough
t
the left hand side approaches
2t(t_{B}+y_{B})
. Hence, if
t_{B}>-y_{B}
there exists a sufficiently large
t
such that (
27
) ceases to hold. Noting that we are in the case
-a\leq x_{B}\leq 0
, so
a^{2}-x_{B}^{2}\geq 0
, if
t_{B}\leq-y_{B}
, the right hand side of (
27
) is always positive. In addition,

\displaystyle 2tt_{B}+2y_{B}\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}}\leq 2t(t_{B}+y_{B})\leq 0\,,

and hence (
27
) holds for all
t
.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

Let
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})=((-a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
,
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})=((a,{\bf 0}_{d-1}),0)
and
({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})=((x_{B},y_{B},{\bf 0}_{d-2}),t_{B})
for some
|x_{B}|\geq a
.
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
if and only if at least one of
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
and
({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
holds.

Proof.

Consider the case
x_{B}\leq-a
(the other case follows symmetrically) and take
y_{B}\geq 0
without loss of generality. We want to maximize (
25
) with respect to
{\bf x}_{d-1}
subject to
|{\bf x}_{d-1}|^{2}\leq t^{2}-a^{2}
. Writing
y=r\cos(\phi)
, i.e.,
r=|{\bf x}_{d-1}|
we can rewrite (
25
) as

t^{2}+x_{B}^{2}+y_{B}^{2}-2y_{B}r\cos(\phi)+a^{2}+2ax_{B}-2(a+x_{B})\sqrt{t^{2}-r^{2}}.

(28)

The maximum of this over
\phi
is when
\cos(\phi)=-1
.

The derivative of (
28
) with respect to
r
is
2r(a+x_{B})/\sqrt{t^{2}-r^{2}}-2y_{B}\cos(\phi)
, which, substituting
\cos(\phi)=-1
is equal to zero for
r=y_{B}t/\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}
. Substituting this into the distance squared gives

\displaystyle(a+x_{B})^{2}\left(1+\frac{t}{\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}}\right)^{2}+y_{B}^{2}\left(1+\frac{t}{\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}}\right)^{2}=\left(t+\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}\right)^{2}.

(29)

This is the maximum provided the value of
r
is within the range of possible
r
values (on which the boundary of
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}
is the same as the boundary of
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}
), i.e., if

\displaystyle\frac{y_{B}t}{\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}}\leq\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}},

which rearranges to

\displaystyle t^{2}\geq\frac{a^{2}y_{B}^{2}}{(a+x_{B})^{2}}+a^{2}.

Hence, for large enough
t
, Eq. (
29
) gives the maximum distance.

If
t^{2}<\frac{a^{2}y_{B}^{2}}{(a+x_{B})^{2}}+a^{2}
, then the maximum occurs on the boundary, i.e., where
r^{2}=t^{2}-a^{2}
. Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
C.5
, the maximum square distance in this case is
x_{B}^{2}+(y_{B}+\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}})^{2}
. Hence
L_{>}({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\big|_{t}\cap L_{>}({\bf x}_{C},t_{C})\big|_{t}\subseteq L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})\big|_{t}

requires

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{B}^{2}+(y_{B}+\sqrt{t^{2}-a^{2}})^{2}\leq(t-t_{B})^{2}&t\leq\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}y_{B}^{2}}{(a+x_{B})^{2}}+a^{2}}\\
\left(t+\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}\right)^{2}\leq(t-t_{B})^{2}&t\geq\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}y_{B}^{2}}{(a+x_{B})^{2}}+a^{2}}\end{cases}.

For the second case to hold for all
t
we require
t_{B}\leq-\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}
. This implies
t_{B}\leq-y_{B}
which means that the first case also holds as shown in the proof of Lemma
C.5
. Noting that
t_{B}\leq-\sqrt{(a+x_{B})^{2}+y_{B}^{2}}
is equivalent to
({\bf x}_{A},t_{A})\in L_{>}({\bf x}_{B},t_{B})
completes the proof.
∎
